On an account Config Studio manages it now runs Config Studio's installer, its «Users» opens Config Studio, and «Remove» says it removes Config Studio's engine and lets the app disconnect from it; the old upload no longer sends `DEBUG`. The old panel's list no longer shows people Config Studio deleted, counts someone online on the engine's own three-minute window, gives a person it creates the `expires_at` Config Studio reads, and moves `updated_at` when it switches someone on or off.

### Added
- **Protocols chosen at creation.** New user, bulk users and edit locations offer VLESS · WebSocket and Trojan · WebSocket (both by default). Each protocol and country gets its own config and credential, and the result lists every config with its protocol, country and exit address.
- **What is left, as the subscription's first entries.** Every `/s/` subscription now opens with entries whose names state the volume left (and today's, under a daily cap) and the time left -- «حجم باقی‌مانده: ۸۷۴ مگابایت از ۱ گیگابایت», «زمان باقی‌مانده: ۳۰ روز» -- worked out from the person's row at each fetch, in the page's own wording and rounding. Clients that ignore `Subscription-Userinfo` show them too, because they are entries. Each is the all-zero id at 127.0.0.1 on its own port, so choosing one goes nowhere and "remove duplicates" cannot fold them. The app reads `Subscription-Userinfo` from any panel, keeps these entries out of its server list, and draws the figures as a card above the subscription's group. Time left is the nearest whole day from a day up (30 on the first day of a 30-day term), hours on the last day, minutes in the last hour -- one rule for the engine's page and entries and for Config Studio, whose user page used to call half a day left "expired".
- **Pull to refresh on every Config Studio page**, through `IosScreen(onRefresh)` and a nested-scroll state for the list pages, since material3 1.1.2 has none.
- **A redesigned Config Studio.** A bottom tab bar (Home, Users, Stats, More) with a gear on Home for settings; one icon family (Material Icons, outlined) with one meaning per glyph; check-mark choices, status lines and two-letter country badges in place of emoji, flags and check/cross characters; inline strings moved into both catalogues.
- **Your own exit server in two minutes.** The Locations page prints a one-line SOCKS5 server command for any VPS, with a random user and password.

## [1.2.35]

### Added
- **GitHub Tunnel («گیت‌هاب تانل») on Android.** The Windows app's system, complete on the phone: a cloud server on the user's own GitHub Actions allowance, reached through a relay Worker on their own Cloudflare account over clean IPs. Device-code sign-in (no password in the app), several accounts with automatic failover when one runs out, make-before-break renewal ahead of the session's end, and «end session» to stop spending minutes. Session credentials come back sealed to a one-time X25519 key generated on the phone (HKDF-SHA256 → AES-256-GCM, bound to the session id) and stay sealed in the private repository; the Worker admits only passes signed with the installation's own secret. The runner, workflow and Worker are the Windows app's own files (`scripts/sync-gt-assets.js`), so both apps can share one GitHub account. Measured on the device: session ready ~35 s after the button, 21–25 Mbit/s down.
- **Exit country.** The runner brings the chosen country's exit up itself (VPN Gate first, Psiphon if that fails) and the connection moves onto it as another VLESS user of the same tunnel — nothing new runs on the phone. The choice is passed to every new session at dispatch, so exits start while the runner boots (four countries ready 28 s after connecting). «What websites see» is measured through the tunnel; the country list is the runner's live catalog with server counts, remembered for before the next session connects.
- **Sites and apps, each from its own country.** Up to five rules at once, each a country with its own sites and/or apps; a site or app lives in one rule only, and a second rule for a country already used is merged into it. Apps are chosen from every package that can use the network (system ones included). Per-app routing uses the bundled core's own hook: `CoreController.registerProcessFinder` with a finder backed by `ConnectivityManager.getConnectionOwnerUid`, matched by Xray `process` rules (UIDs), limited to the tun inbound so the lookup is never paid for proxy clients. Such a config needs the tun's real destination kept (`routeOnly`), and with it IPv6 is left unconfigured so Android blocks the family — the runner has no IPv6, and every v6 site failed with the connection closed until then. Verified on the device, all at once: ip.me from Korea, ifconfig.co from Germany, Chrome from the US, everything else from Japan; site rules win over app rules. Known limit: Cloudflare sites in Chrome use ECH, which hides the name a site rule matches on.
- **UDP, labelled and measured.** Every country and exit says whether it carries UDP (VPN Gate does; Psiphon does not, and there UDP leaves from the runner's own address). A «UDP (calls, games)» row sends one STUN binding request through the tunnel's own SOCKS inbound (UDP ASSOCIATE) and shows the address it was really seen from.
- **A new Game Booster: pick a game, press Boost, and see what was measured.** It checks each part of the game from your own line -- sign-in, the game's services, Google sign-in -- and says what is in the way: nothing, a lying DNS, a filter, or the publisher refusing Iran (a 403/451 with the host's own certificate). A sign-in refused on country is opened with no setup: the Iranian anti-sanction DNS services (Shecan, 403, Electro, Radar, Begzar) are each tried on the line, a provider counts only where the refusal is really gone, and only the refused names are sent to it -- the match itself stays on the phone's own route, untouched (a DNS-only session; everything else resolves through the line's own resolvers). The card names the kind of game this is on this line -- open, sanctioned, filtered, partly blocked, or both -- in one sentence. Every region of the game is measured at once and the card says which one to pick inside the game: the Gulf datacentres (AWS Bahrain and UAE) that hosted most Middle East servers were struck in March 2026, Bahrain is marked down, and Europe is the default. The line is measured by UDP echo from AWS's GameLift beacons spread over many flows (a beacon answers about two packets a second per flow, so one flow read as loss that was not there), and by TCP handshakes where UDP gets no answer. Cloudflare WARP is measured beside it in the same seconds and used only when it beats the direct line by the desktop booster's rules, or when the game's own sign-in cannot be reached any other way -- test points that do not answer are never a reason to tunnel (on Irancell they are silent while games work); the card offers «امتحان با WARP» instead. WARP never replaces a working sign-in fix, since WARP does not lift sanctions. The whole run has a hard 25-second budget. Also: phone checks with a fix button, now including background downloads measured while the booster runs (on mobile data it suggests Data Saver, which holds back background apps and leaves the game alone) and Bluetooth audio on 2.4 GHz Wi-Fi; Wi-Fi power saving off for the session; a «بوست و اجرا» home-screen icon per game that boosts and then opens it; games that are not installed can be measured; per-network memory; a live ping in the notification; any installed app as a game; and an Advanced page. The previous booster stays one tap away as «نسخهٔ قبلی».
- **The Game Booster learns.** Each session ends with a verdict drawn from what could be observed -- how long the route stayed up, how much of it the live reading was bad, a quick stop -- and now and then one question, «بازی خوب کار کرد؟», which weighs three sessions. This phone keeps those outcomes per network, game and route, fading with a two-week half-life, and a route that kept failing on a network is left for the other one next time (never into WARP when signing in needs the direct line, never out of WARP when only WARP reaches the game). With sharing on (default; a switch in Advanced), a sample of sessions sends one anonymous row -- game, region, route, worked or not, typical ping, the kind of game on that line, which anti-sanction DNS opened its sign-in -- to a small Cloudflare Worker that keeps only counters per operator (ASN read from Cloudflare's own data; no address or device id) and serves back a snapshot per operator, signed with Ed25519 and verified by the app. Players on the same operator then start from what worked: the anti-sanction DNS that opened this game there is tried first and ones that never do are skipped, WARP is not measured where it almost never wins, the card says how a route did for others there, and the region to pick comes from them when the line's own test points are silent. The owner can switch features off or change the reporting rate for everyone from the Worker. Also new: sign-in hosts filtered on their TLS name are opened by sending the connection in pieces (proven from the phone first with a hand-driven handshake, then only those addresses go through a fragmenting outbound -- the Iran profiles' own); on lines that drop foreign UDP (Irancell) WARP is measured and used over TCP; each lag spike during play is traced to what moved with it -- another app downloading, the Wi-Fi signal or link rate, the phone's heat, or the line itself -- shown live and summed up after the session with the one fix that matches; a floating ping over the game (taps pass through); a «گیم بوست» quick-settings tile; «چه مشکلی داری؟» for can't-sign-in, high ping, lag, hit registration, disconnects and stuck updates, each running the thorough check that problem needs; and on Wi-Fi with mobile data up behind it, the card says when mobile data is the faster line for the game.
- **Endpoints, in the app.** «نقطه‌های اتصال» — the clean addresses a subscription is served on — could be written by the engine since build 6 and by nothing else: `/v1/nodes` existed, was tested, and had not one line of client code. There is now a screen: address, port list, country, server name, Host header and priority, with the address LTR-pinned like every other payload in this feature. A **new** endpoint offers to write itself to every connected Cloudflare account, because one clean IP is normally wanted wherever a person was placed; an **edit** addresses only the account that holds it, because writing an edit fleet-wide would create copies on accounts that never had the row.
- **An endpoint test that runs on the phone.** "Test" opens a TCP connection to the endpoint from the device, on the network the operator is actually on, and posts the result to that endpoint's installation (`POST /v1/nodes/{id}:health`). A worker cannot answer this question: it would be timing Cloudflare's own path to the address, and what matters is whether the address answers *from Iran*. The result is stored as three states, not two — answering, not answering, **not tested** — because an endpoint nobody has probed is not a broken one, and colouring it red sends the operator to replace something that works. The failure keeps its own words: "refused", "unreachable" and "timed out" call for three different actions and one translated «خطا» hides which it was. A reported latency is clamped to 60 s rather than believed, so a client clock skew cannot put a six-digit figure on screen.
- **The dashboard counts it could not before.** People connected right now, live configs, endpoints and how many are down, connections closed today, and the month's traffic. Each is index-covered rather than a scan: `ix_users_last_active` for the first, a **partial** index over live rows for configs, and 720 hourly buckets for the month whatever the user count. **Every one of them draws a dash, not a zero, on an installation that cannot answer it** — an engine on build 6 sends no such field, and a tile reading the absence as zero would tell an operator with fifty people connected that nobody is.
- **Shortcuts on the first screen**, starting with «کاربر تازه». Adding someone is the thing this app is opened to do and it lived one level down, inside the user list.
- **The activity log, finally written.** `activity_log` has existed since the first Config Studio migration and **nothing had ever inserted a row into it** — so "recent errors" was a screen with no source. The data plane now records each refusal with the reason that caused it: out of volume, out of today's volume, term ended, switched off, a credential nobody owns, and each of the three caps the Durable Object enforces. Written at most **once per user per kind per hour**, because a client being refused does not stop retrying and a row per refusal would let one expired subscriber spend the account's whole daily write budget by leaving their phone on; pruned to 4,000 rows from inside the writer, since a Worker has no scheduler. `GET /v1/activity` reads it, keyset-paged, resolving each uid to a name; the dashboard shows the last few as «مشکل‌های اخیر» with the full log one tap away.
- **What a Cloudflare credential may actually do**, proved by the cheapest read each permission needs rather than parsed out of the token — a Global API Key has no policy document, and a token's permission group can change under it. A 403 is reported as refused; a timeout or a 404 is reported as **no answer**, because telling an operator their token lacks a permission it has sends them to reissue a working credential.
- **A custom domain for the engine.** Type `vpn.example.com` and the zone that owns it is found on the account (longest matching suffix, on a label boundary, so `notexample.com` cannot match `example.com`). The workers.dev address keeps working — a custom domain is added beside it, never instead of it, because every subscription link already handed out points at the old one.
- **Install again, and remove.** «نصب دوباره» redeploys over whatever is there including a newer build, which Repair deliberately will not do; «حذف موتور» deletes the worker from the Cloudflare account, with the D1 database as a separate opt-in inside the same confirmation. Script first, database second: a database deleted while a worker still binds it leaves an address that answers every request with a D1 error, which reads as a broken tunnel rather than an uninstalled one.
- **Does the installation answer from here.** The setup scan asks Cloudflare, which will happily confirm a worker the operator's own ISP has blocked. This asks the address directly, from the device, and reports the camouflage page and the control plane separately — one answering without the other is a route problem, neither answering is a reachability problem, and a single "is it up" flag would collapse them into an unactionable no.
- **The dashboard's missing tiles and shortcuts**: people whose term has ended (a figure the engine had always sent and nothing drew), and «کانفیگ تازه برای…», which reuses the user list as a picker rather than growing a second screen with its own copy of the search.

- **A config builder.** The two-shape dialog is gone. Protocol and transport, TLS fingerprint, ALPN, the path the config answers on, and a credential that can be typed instead of generated — then a review step showing exactly what will be made, because once a config exists somebody may already be holding its link and undoing it means reaching them. **Only what the engine can serve is offered**, and every absence has a reason in the data plane rather than in the model: gRPC, raw TCP and HTTP Upgrade are not implementable as Worker inbounds; VMess and Shadowsocks have working URI builders here and no server side, so offering them would produce links that import cleanly into any client and never connect. XHTTP *is* offered, with its three constraints enforced rather than discovered — it authenticates against the person's own id, it cannot claim a path, and it bills one request per upload chunk.
- **Address, port and server name are named as endpoint properties instead of being offered as dead fields.** The engine renders one link per config per endpoint per port, so a host typed on a config would be overwritten by every node in the list; the builder says where they live. DNS and routing are absent for a different reason and it is stated: a `vless://` URI has no field for either — they are settings in the client that receives the link.
- **A copy of an existing config**, same shape and same handshake, with its own credential and its own path — sharing those would be one config twice, revocable only together, which is the opposite of why a second one is made.
- **A transport test before publishing**, and an honest one. It cannot dial the config — the path does not exist until the config does, and the engine serves the camouflage page to every unclaimed path. What it checks is the half that actually fails in Iran: a carrier that kills WebSocket drops the *upgrade* while ordinary requests keep working, so a config built on top of it never connects and nothing says why. The probe sends the real upgrade headers, from the device, dialling by address and presenting the worker's name — the pairing a clean IP exists for.
- **A way to hand somebody their link that is not the clipboard.** A QR code, the Android share sheet, and a saved file, beside the Copy that was the only exit from this screen. The QR says why there is none when the link is larger than a QR can hold — a subscription carrying a config per endpoint per port is past three kilobytes — rather than drawing a square that scans into nothing. The file is written into the cache and handed over through the existing `FileProvider`, never left in Downloads: a file full of live subscription links is a copy nobody remembers to delete.
- **Tags and a private note on a person.** Both columns have existed since the first Config Studio migration and **nothing had ever written to either**, so every installation in the fleet was carrying an empty `note` and an empty `tags` for everybody in it. Tags become the filter row above the user list; neither appears on the subscriber's own page or reaches the client that reads the link. They are edited on the user's page rather than at creation, because what an operator knows about somebody is learned after handing them a link.
- **The user list can be ordered and narrowed.** Six orders — newest, oldest, by name, expiring soonest, heaviest use, last connected — and seven conditions, including three the engine never writes into a column: expiry is evaluated when somebody connects rather than by a sweep, so reading "expired" off `status` would draw an empty list on an installation full of expired people. Never-expiring rows sort to the END of "expiring soonest" rather than the beginning, where SQLite's NULL ordering would otherwise put the people who will never need attention. The search now covers name, tag, note and id together instead of the name alone, and a count above the list says how many the narrowing actually matched.
- **A renewal can be taken back, and the usage meter can be zeroed on its own.** `:renew` accepts negative days and volume, which is the only way to undo a mistyped figure without deleting the person — and deleting them loses their link, the one thing the whole renewal flow exists to protect. The expiry floors at now rather than landing in 1970, the quota floors at what has already been used (a quota under the meter refuses them forever, including after the next top-up), and a reduction does **not** switch a stopped subscriber back on. Zeroing the meter is a third mode rather than a flag on restart, because "the volume was spent by something that was not them" must not also move the expiry. The engine advertises `renew.adjust`; where an account has not been updated, neither control is drawn — build 7 takes a negative figure unclamped and reads the new mode as an ordinary renewal, and both failures are silent.
- **Doing one thing to many people.** «انتخاب» in the user list turns on checkmarks; the selection then goes to a page of its own where it can be switched on or off, renewed, reduced, capped, collected as links, or deleted. **Every action reports per person, never as one verdict** — nineteen of twenty succeeding is the ordinary outcome across a fleet (an account unreachable, a name deleted from another device) and a single "failed" would hide which nineteen worked. It does not stop at the first failure, and it is sequential rather than concurrent: twenty parallel requests to one worker is twenty concurrent D1 transactions on a free plan, to save a second. The loop is on the client because a selection is not a shard — twenty people chosen off one list can live on twenty different Cloudflare accounts, so a `users:bulk` endpoint would only move the loop somewhere that can see one of them.
- **The three caps, changed across a selection, without clearing the other two.** A cap field has three intentions and `Int?` carries two: leave it alone, set it, remove it. An empty box leaves that cap exactly as it is and `0` removes it, so changing the device limit on two hundred people cannot silently wipe their connection and IP limits — which is what three boxes that all get written would do every time.
- **Many users at once, from a prefix and a count.** Numbers are zero-padded to one width so the list stays in order wherever it is pasted, a preview shows the first name, the last and the count on every keystroke, and a name already taken anywhere in the fleet is flagged **while typing** rather than nineteen creations in. Each person costs the same three calls one person does, in the same order and for the same reason: create, attach a config, then read the link back — a subscription with no config resolves to an empty document, HTTP 200 with zero servers, which imports cleanly and never connects. Somebody whose config failed is therefore reported by name instead of being handed a link. At the end: copy the whole list, share it, save it as a file, or open one QR at a time. Capped at 500 a run, which is a guard against a slipped digit rather than a technical ceiling.
- **«قالب» — a config shape, named and reused.** The `templates` table has existed since the first Config Studio migration and **not one row was ever written into it**. It has an endpoint now, fleet-synced under a client-chosen id exactly as a «بسته» is, with the same drift line for an account that missed a write. A plan is what somebody is *allowed*; a template is what their link *looks like* — merging them would mean a new row every time either half changed. A template is a **starting point, never a live reference**: a config copies the shape when it is made and carries its own URI template afterwards, so editing one changes nothing for anybody already holding a link, and there is deliberately no `:apply` — re-shaping an existing config means re-issuing its credential, which is a per-person action on purpose. Deleting one is a real delete rather than an archive, because nothing points at a template after the fact; any plan naming it is released in the same batch.
- **Four starting points that are actually different on the wire.** Steady (VLESS over WebSocket, one request held open for the whole connection), Phone (Trojan, which sends a hash rather than a uuid, with an iOS handshake), Game, and Upgrades-blocked (XHTTP, with its request cost on the label). Game is the same *shape* as Steady and says so: **nothing in a `vless://` URI makes a connection faster**, so inventing a "gaming protocol" would be a label over nothing. What that preset is for is the endpoint list — which is the one choice here that changes the latency a player feels.
- **A config can be served on chosen endpoints rather than all of them.** `configs.node_selector` and the template's own selector: the subscription fan-out now renders each config only on the endpoints it names. A config naming endpoints that no longer exist falls back to **all** of them rather than to none, because a config that renders zero links is a subscription entry that vanishes and "the endpoint was deleted" is not something the person holding the link can see or act on.
- **`Plan.templateId` finally does something.** It has been in the schema, on the wire and in the domain model since the beginning, and nothing had ever set it — every plan ever made here carried a null. The plan editor picks a shape now, and creating somebody on that plan (one at a time or five hundred at a time) builds their config from it. The builder gained both directions too: start from a stored shape, or keep what you just built as one. And a whole selection can be given a config from a shape — **added, never replacing**, so every existing link keeps working.

- **«ترکیب با آی‌پی تمیز» — an assistant that puts one person on an address that answers.** The most useful thing this app does was, for a Studio subscriber, not possible at all: a clean IP could be added as an endpoint for *everybody*, and there was no way to say "serve **this** person on **that** address". The assistant is three steps and does the work rather than narrating it — candidates come from the scanner's own archive, from a sweep started inside the assistant, or typed; each is tried the way the subscriber's client will try it (connect to the address, hand shake under the engine's own server name, ask for one page), and only an address that answered can be picked. **The subscription link does not change and neither does the credential**, which is the property that makes this usable on somebody who is currently stuck: nothing is re-sent, nobody is contacted, and every device they already set up keeps working. It is reachable from the person's own page and from the dashboard through a picker, because the operator who needs it is usually looking at a complaint rather than at that person.
- **`POST /v1/configs/{id}:nodes`**, the engine half of that. Separate from `:rotate` for exactly the reason above — a retarget that re-issued the secret would disconnect the person it was meant to rescue. A selector naming endpoints the installation does not have is **refused**, not stored: an unknown selector falls back to every endpoint at read time, so accepting one would look like success on the operator's screen while the address they were moving away from carried on being served. Both sides go into the audit log, because an endpoint id is not a secret and "what was it pointed at before" is the first question asked when a retarget makes things worse. An empty list puts the config back on every endpoint, so the undo is as cheap as the do.
- **The subscription link opens as a page when a person taps it.** One link, two audiences: a VPN client fetching `/s/{token}` still gets the base64 server list, and a browser gets a status page — what is left, how long it lasts, how many devices are connected right now, how many servers the link carries, and a one-tap handover into v2rayNG, Hiddify, sing-box, Clash, Streisand or Shadowrocket. **`Sec-Fetch-Mode: navigate` is the signal**, not a user-agent guess: every browser since 2020 sends it on a top-level navigation and no subscription fetch does, so it is the browser saying "a person tapped this" rather than an inference about the client. `?format=raw` and `?format=base64` always win in one direction and `?page=1` in the other. Handing the operator two links to keep straight is how the wrong one gets sent.
- **What the status page can now say.** Live device count read from the Durable Object that enforces the cap — so the number shown and the number doing the refusing cannot disagree — the daily allowance where there is one, when they last connected, and how many servers the link actually carries. Everything the installation cannot measure is still drawn as nothing at all rather than as a zero: an engine without the object shows the allowance and no count, because "nobody has connected" and "this installation cannot count" look identical in a `۰`.


- **What actually happened, per connection.** `sessions` was created by the third Config Studio migration and **nothing had ever inserted a row into it**, so three separate screens had a table and no data. One row per closed connection now answers all three: a person's own connection history, traffic per config, and which protocol and transport are really in use. It goes in the same batch as the closing byte flush — one more row write, no extra round trip — and it is written **even when the connection carried nothing**, which is the row worth having: "it connects and nothing happens" is what an operator is actually looking at, and the whole close path used to sit behind a bytes-greater-than-zero guard that made exactly that session invisible to every report, the hourly connection counter included.
- **Five analytics endpoints and the screens that read them.** People added, people who connected, connections, refusals and the refusal rate; traffic broken down by config, by protocol and by transport; connections over time, narrowable to one person; and new people by the day. The refusal rate is out of **attempts** rather than out of connections — a refused attempt never became one, so the obvious denominator reports zero per cent for a period in which everything was refused.
- **Real charts, with the ends of the axis labelled**, for traffic, connections and new people — drawn left to right in time regardless of layout direction, because a mirrored time axis is not a translation of a chart but a different claim about the same data.
- **A person's page says what they actually did**: their connection history and, beside it, only the refusals that were theirs. In that order, because the pair is diagnostic — connections and no refusals is a network problem, refusals is an account problem, and connections that carried nothing is a config problem. The empty ones are named rather than shown as a zero, since `۰` reads as "used a little".
- **There is no per-endpoint traffic figure, and the screen says why rather than leaving a gap.** A Cloudflare Worker never learns which address the client dialled — the edge routes by server name and the address it was reached on is in neither the request nor `request.cf`. That is a measurement this architecture cannot make, not a feature that has not been built, and the two look identical as an absence.
- **An errors-only filter on the dashboard feed.** The default still keeps warnings, because a refusal for a spent quota is what an operator most often needs and it is a warning — but on an account with many expired subscribers those are a steady background with the one genuinely broken row underneath.

- **The combine hands off to the app's own scanner instead of carrying one.** Picking somebody in Config Studio now reads their configs, arms a handoff and opens the scanner — where the archive, the per-address measurement and the combine sheet the operator already uses are waiting. In that sheet the subscriber appears at the top of the sources, badged «کانفیگ استدیو» and showing how many configs they have, because a cloud group and a subscriber both read as "a set of configs with a name" and combining the wrong one produces a group that looks right and belongs to somebody else. The earlier version had a sweep of its own; it worked, and it was a second scanner with a second archive and a second set of measurements to keep honest.
- **«بروزرسانی ساب» on a combined group.** Only on a group combined FOR a subscriber — every other group has no subscription to update and no way to guess whose it would be. It does not upload the links: a Studio subscription is rendered by the engine from the config rows crossed with the endpoint list, so the addresses become endpoints and the person's configs are pointed at them. Same links on screen, and the subscription URL and every credential unchanged — which is what makes it safe to press on somebody who is connected right now. Uploading the links instead would bake a host into each config's template, and the next clean IP would then need every config re-issued rather than one endpoint edited. It publishes the measured addresses where a health test has run, and existing endpoints with the same address are reused rather than duplicated.
- **The dashboard's shortcuts are a grid.** Three rows of three, grouped by subject — people, links, the fleet — instead of eleven identical rows. Eleven rows is a menu read top to bottom every time; three rows of three is a shape learned once and then hit by position. Refresh and settings stay as ordinary rows underneath, because putting a utility in the grid would give it the same weight as «کاربر تازه».
### Fixed
- **An exit that silently left from the runner's own address.** A VPN Gate exit stayed «ready» while every byte through it left from the runner's Azure address; the runner's health check only tested liveness. The runner now measures each exit's egress every minute and rebuilds one that leaves from its own address or another country, never picking that VPN Gate server again in the session (with the tunnel's state logged for diagnosis); the app, seeing the same, says so, moves traffic onto the runner's address openly and asks for the rebuild (`/x/stop?…&leak=1`). Shared runner code — the Windows app gets it too.
- **Country pickers follow the Persian alphabet.** Names were sorted by code point, which put «ژاپن», «کره» and «کانادا» after «ویتنام»; they are now sorted with the locale's collator.
- **The quick-settings tile could replay a game session for the whole phone.** A game connect recorded itself as the last config, so the tile's next tap brought the game's per-app config back for every app. Game sessions are no longer recorded, the auto-switch monitor stays off during them, and an ordinary connect now clears a leftover game-mode flag instead of inheriting it.
- **Gemini said "not available in your country" on Cloudflare configs.** A worker dials what it is handed, and the tunnel handed it names (the sniffer replaces every destination with the TLS name); given a name, Cloudflare's `connect()` prefers IPv6, so Google saw the worker's shared WARP IPv6 exit — and answered every Gemini request from it with its "unusual traffic" page, which the app shows as a country block. Measured through a live panel config on the phone: by name, blocked every time; by IPv4 address, the same worker left from its IPv4 exit and got the full Gemini page. Google's domains now go through a copy of the tunnel with `targetStrategy: UseIPv4`, their names resolved over DoH *through* the tunnel (so an ISP-poisoned answer can never be handed to the worker), IPv4 only — NetDoctor's behaviour, whose sing-box resolves remotely `ipv4_only` and never gives its worker a name. YouTube is left where it was. Also: QUIC to a worker is now refused at once instead of silently dropped (a loopback responder answering with a QUIC Version Negotiation packet; ~20 ms instead of 4 s of retransmits in desktop Chrome). Both apply to share-link worker configs, to imported whole-JSON worker configs and to anti-sanction's worker leg, behind one switch in Settings, «رفع مشکل جمنای و برنامه‌های گوگل»; off is the old behaviour exactly.
- **«ترکیب» handed the scanner a subscriber it then refused to use.** The handover armed correctly and the scanner's combine sheet knew how to offer the person — and between the two, nothing connected them. The scanner opened with an **empty base config**, so the operator had to go and pick one by hand from a picker that lists cloud groups and has never heard of Config Studio; and when the scan finished, «ترکیب» checked `cloudGroups.isEmpty()` before opening its sheet, so an operator with a Studio subscriber armed and no cloud group saved was told there was no group to combine with — the one case the whole handover exists for. The armed subscriber's own config now fills the base-config field on arrival and is pre-selected in the sheet, and either a cloud group **or** an armed subscriber is enough to open it. «بروزرسانی ساب» also moved onto the group's card, beside «انتقال»: it existed only inside the dialog behind the eye icon, which is not where the operator is standing after a health test.
- **Every VLESS config with a credential of its own produced a link that could never connect.** The WebSocket path authenticated with `SELECT * FROM users WHERE uuid = ?` and never looked at `configs` — but the builder generates a per-config credential for every shape that can carry one, which is the default for VLESS over WebSocket, the most common config this product makes. That credential matched no row, so the connection was refused as an unknown one. It failed silently and expensively from both ends: the operator sees a config in the list, the subscriber sees a server in their app, the link imports cleanly, and nothing anywhere says the secret is not one the engine knows. Trojan never had the problem, because its lookup has always joined `configs`. The lookup now tries the config first and the user second — the `users.uuid` branch is not legacy tolerance but a requirement, since XHTTP authenticates against the user's own uuid, every config written before per-config credentials carries it in that column, and an adopted installation has users with no configs row at all.
- **The same bug dropped healthy sessions on the fourth heartbeat.** The periodic cap re-check re-read the user by the credential on the wire, so a per-config credential found nobody — which that block reads as "the account is gone". It reads by uid now, which is the primary key and is already in hand from admission.
- **Rotating a config's credential quietly reset its handshake.** The rotate path rebuilt the URI template from the config's shape alone, so a config made with a non-default fingerprint or ALPN came back from a rotation as a plain default one. Harmless while the builder offered no such choices and wrong the moment it did. The stored template is reused instead — the credential is a `{{cred}}` placeholder, so rotating never needed to touch it.
- **Endpoint groups would not compile.** Forty-eight string keys were used across the four endpoint-group screens and defined in neither catalogue, so `:app:compileDebugKotlin` failed on every one of them. `scripts/check-studio.py` now also refuses an **unescaped apostrophe** in a string resource — aapt rejects the whole file for one, names the resource rather than the character, calls it an "invalid unicode escape sequence", and does it during resource merging, so the build dies before a line of Kotlin is compiled and the message points at the wrong thing.
- **The second page of the activity log returned 503.** Its query joins `users` to resolve a uid to a name, and `users` has an `id` column of its own — so the unqualified `id < ?` in the keyset clause was ambiguous and SQLite refused the whole statement. It only appeared on the second page, because the first passes no cursor and never builds the clause. Caught by the worker test harness before it shipped.
- **Every connection count in the product read zero.** `usage_hourly.sessions` was created by the first Config Studio migration and incremented by nothing, so the figure existed in the schema, in the analytics query and on no screen. It is now written on the same upsert that was already flushing bytes — no extra D1 write — and a session that ends having moved *no* bytes is counted too, which the obvious version of this fix would still have missed: that path returns before the write.
- **Config Studio's screens did not look like the rest of the app.** Nine places put a `SettingsFooter` — the grey caption iOS puts *under* a card — *inside* one, which double-indented the text and pressed it flat against the bottom edge; the user and account lists filled their rows with the opaque fallback colour instead of glass, the only two cards in the app drawn that way; forty-four grouped cards carried not one row separator where the rest of the app has 209; the audit list had no card at all and scrolled as text lying on the wallpaper; three pushed pages printed their own name a second time in 22 sp under the navigation bar already showing it; two filter chips had different corner radii and a near-black label on a blue fill; and thirty hand-written spacers sat on top of the 26 dp a section header already owns, so sections were half again as far apart as everywhere else. The subscription link, worker address and route paths were bare monospace text on the wallpaper and are now on cards. Also removed: a sentence printed twice on the "user ready" screen, and a column of route paths repeated under the config list that could not say which config each belonged to.
- **The bulk-add paste box was one line tall** for a value that is one account per line.
- **«ترکیب» in Config Studio armed the scanner and then abandoned the operator on it.** The handover screen filled the base-config field, pre-selected the subscriber in the combine sheet, and stopped there — the assistant that walks exactly this job from «ابری» was never started for a Studio subscriber. So the operator arrived on a scanner with the right config in it and nothing saying that combining, measuring and writing the subscription were all still theirs to find, in that order, across three dialogs. It could not have worked even if it had started: step 3 resolved its source only from cloud groups, and a Studio subscriber is not one, so it would have stopped on "there is nothing to combine" against a person whose configs were sitting in the handoff. The assistant now travels with the handover and does the whole of it — the only thing left to the operator is choosing what the scan optimises for and how many clean addresses to find. It **names the subscriber on every step**, because an operator fixing several people in a sitting is one mis-click from publishing to the wrong one, and it adds a fourth step the cloud version does not have: the measured addresses are written into that person's subscription, which is the entire reason the feature exists. A failed publish stops the guide on an orange bar and leaves «بروزرسانی ساب» on the group's card to retry; the guide's own last bar no longer restarts itself when the next scan begins, which would have published the same person twice.
- **A Studio combine put a stranger's configs into the operator's own connection list.** The step that transfers what it measured is right for a cloud combine — those configs are for the person holding the phone — and wrong for one done on somebody else's behalf. It is skipped for a Studio group; those addresses go into the subscription instead.
- **The link on the subscriber's own page was the address of the page itself.** «لینک اشتراک» printed the bare `/s/{token}`, which is the URL already in the address bar of anyone who reached the page by opening their link in a browser — the ordinary way a person opens a link they were sent. So the section that exists to hand over a subscription handed over the status page, and the obvious conclusion is that no subscription link was ever given. It now prints `?format=base64`, which is read **before** the browser-versus-client guess and forces the list, so the link the page offers imports whoever fetches it and however they do it — including the clients that fetch through a WebView with a borrowed browser agent, which the guess alone would answer with a page of HTML where a parser expected servers. The one-tap app buttons carry the same string. Engine build 14; existing installations report as behind so the redeploy is offered rather than left to be discovered.


## [1.2.34] — 2026-09-12

### Fixed
- **The SNI engine was given one of its five levers, and the wrong value of it.** `-method` was hardcoded to `combined`, which throws away the argument the caller passed — and the engine also takes `-fragment-strategy` (`sni_split` | `half` | `chunk` | `record_frag`, defaulting to the first), `-fragment-delay`, `-no-raw` and `-ttl-trick`. On one Iranian mobile network `sni_split` fragmentation is exactly what the DPI kills: measured three attempts each against one edge under one forged name, the shipping settings carried **zero bytes every time**, `fragment`+`chunk` answered **every time**, and with no engine at all the line answered every time — so the line was healthy and the setting was the fault. There are now 14 named methods covering the lever space, the measure button walks them until one answers and keeps it, and the one that already worked stays first so a network that was connecting is tested first. No operator is named anywhere: what is chosen comes from measuring the line in front of it.
- **`stopLocked()` destroyed the claim on the front that `acquire()` had just made**, because it zeroed the ownership count and `startLocked()` calls it first to clear the way. Everything built on that count then failed quietly: `release()` saw nobody holding a front that was plainly running, so the process outlived every measurement — the stray engine that lights the SNI lamp over somebody else's tunnel, which the count exists to prevent — and the method ladder's restart became a no-op, so **half its rungs were measured against the previous rung's settings**, and the rungs proven to work on the failing network were both in the half that never ran. The outright stop still clears the count, in `stop()`, where it belongs.
- **`withTimeoutOrNull` around the delay measurement was not a timeout.** It cancels at a suspension point and a blocking call into the core is not one. Measured on the device: three test instances logged `core: Xray started` and then **returned nothing for over three minutes**, on a 12-second timeout. Nothing after it ever ran — the list never filled, the spinner never stopped, and the engine was never released, which is the whole of a measurement that sits on its loading indicator for ever. The call now runs on a thread that can be abandoned and the wait is on a Deferred, which is a real suspension point. Three other panels still carry the same trap and are noted in the source.
- **The time budget was shorter than the handshake it was timing.** One WebSocket upgrade through the fragmented front takes **9 to 14 seconds** on an Iranian mobile line — confirmed by `101 Switching Protocols` from the worker itself, on all four routes tried, twice each. At 7 and 12 seconds every such config reported "no ping" while being perfectly able to connect. It is now 20 seconds to probe and 25 to measure, each config gets a second attempt before being called dead, and they run two at a time rather than three: they all pass through one engine and crowding it spoils the reading. Shortening the fragment delay does not help — 0.02s and 0.005s measured no faster than the default, because the slowness is the line.
- **The config measurement counted past the number of configs and left its spinner turning.** The second stage was a copy of the first and shared its counter, so eight configs counted as far as 16; and the copy exited by a path that skipped the line clearing the spinner. It is one function now, making its own counter each time, with the flag cleared where no exit can skip it.
- **The entry-point test button sat under a list of twenty-eight**, so the one thing to press on a network where the selected route is not the one that works was behind a scroll. It is above the list, and the button itself is no longer a second copy of the scan the connect button runs.
- **One bundled sample config could never load**: its address was typed `127.0.0..1`.

## [1.2.33] — 2026-09-06

### Added
- **Config Studio.** A product inside the app that installs on the operator's own Cloudflare account: create people, hand each one a subscription link, and control the volume and the time on it. The engine is the existing worker extended in place — one worker, one D1 — so an operator who already ran the panel is *adopted* rather than migrated, with no data movement at all.
  Installing from a second phone now **finds the existing installation instead of minting a second worker**. The old deployer derived its script name from a URL stored on the phone, so a device that had never deployed fell through to a random name and Cloudflare cheerfully created a second engine beside the first — serving the old script on the old address, outside the staleness check, never upgraded again, and still the target of every link already handed out. The name is now derived from the Cloudflare account itself, so any device recomputes the same one. A scan that *fails* refuses to deploy at all: not being able to see an account is precisely the state in which installing does damage.
  The subscription carries `Subscription-Userinfo`, so volume and expiry render in the person's own client with no browser. `expire` is UNIX **seconds** where the rest of the schema is milliseconds — handing a client milliseconds puts the expiry fifty thousand years out, renders fine, and is wrong. `total=0` means unlimited and `expire=0` means never, because omitting them makes clients render nothing at all, which reads as "broken" rather than "unlimited". `/p/{token}` is a Persian RTL page with volume, time, and, when someone is cut off, **the reason in a sentence**; it never draws a number this installation cannot measure, and the link on it is LTR-pinned because an RTL container reorders an ASCII URL both on screen and in what gets copied.
  **Renewing does not change the token.** The person holding the link does nothing and is not contacted, and an early renewal extends from the current expiry rather than from today.
- **Plans.** One set of terms — volume, duration, daily volume, device/connection/IP limits, reset policy, and whether the clock starts at creation or at first connection — written once and chosen when adding someone. Choosing one **fills the fields rather than hiding them**. Editing a plan changes nothing for the people already on it, because their terms are copied at creation, not referenced; pushing an edit onto them is a separate confirmed action that deliberately leaves volume and expiry alone. Plans are written to every account under one client-chosen id, so the write is an idempotent `PUT` that is safe to retry, and a plan missing from an account says so on its row.
  **No commerce vocabulary and no commerce column, in any phase.** `checkStringParity` enforces it over both string catalogues **and** `worker-src/studio/**`, including the migrations — a `price` column is exactly the thing that later grows a screen.
- **A fleet of Cloudflare accounts, uncapped.** Every ceiling is per account, so more accounts is the only thing that raises them. Settings › Accounts lists the fleet with search, per-shard health, engine build and a capacity meter; **Add several** connects a pasted list in one pass, running the same discovery scan per account so a duplicate paste adopts rather than deploys, and failing one account without ending the queue.
  **Placement is automatic and shown before it is committed**, not after: it decides which worker serves that person's link. The emptiest healthy account wins, anything unreachable or on an older engine is skipped, and an account whose load has never been read does not win by default — "unknown" and "empty" are the same number and only one of them is a reason to send everyone there.
  The capacity meter is **an estimate and says so**: Cloudflare's request counter cannot be read from inside a Worker, and counting requests in D1 would spend one row write per request to measure the budget it was spending.
- **A contact line and a name for the subscriber's page**, set per installation. Both were already read by `/p/{token}` and neither could be written by anything, so the contact line the design promised on an expired subscription could never appear. `/v1/settings` is an allowlist rather than a key/value store — the same table holds `panel_password` and every burned bootstrap hash, and a generic write would turn a spent deploy secret back into a live one.
- **Device, concurrent-connection and IP limits are enforced**, via a `SessionDO` Durable Object — one object per user, so the count and the admission are one serialized turn. KV cannot do this (eventually consistent: two colos both read zero and both admit) and a D1 leases table cannot afford it (~480 row writes for one eight-hour session, so twelve people exhaust the daily budget). A device already seen does not consume a new slot, two connections from one address are one address, and no raw client IP is stored — only a salted, truncated hash, enough to count distinct addresses and not enough to be a subscriber-address list on someone else's account. Device recognition is a heuristic and every surface says so.
  A **per-user strict/soft switch** rides the same code path: soft records the lease, the device and the counts and refuses nothing. That is also what an installation with no Durable Object does, so the fallback was the design rather than a workaround.
  **The deploy cannot fail because of it.** R1's metadata — a `durable_object_namespace` binding plus a tagged migration, with `new_sqlite_classes` sent once and the tag replayed as `{"old_tag":"v1","new_tag":"v1"}` afterwards — has never been prototyped against a real account, so an upload carrying it that fails is retried without it and produces a working installation in soft mode. Taking an operator's tunnel down to add a limit is worse than the limit going unapplied. `studioDoTag` is recorded only on a deploy that actually carried the object.
- **`GET /v1/users/{id}/devices`** and `:block` / `:unblock` / `:forget`, answered by the object that enforces the cap rather than from a table — anything else would be a second number that can disagree with the one doing the refusing. An installation without the object answers `available: false`, which is a third state and not an empty list: "nobody has connected" and "this installation cannot count" would otherwise look identical.
- **`on_first_connect` expiry works.** Until the link is used the user has no `expires_at` at all, which is the point of the mode — a link sitting unopened must not burn its own time. One D1 write, once in a user's life, on the connection that starts them.
- **Quota reset policies work.** `quota_reset_policy` was a column the operator could set, the subscriber page could describe and nothing acted on — and Phase 2 had just added the UI for it. The next boundary advances from the stored one rather than from now, so a user who does not connect for two months lands back on the same day of the cycle instead of dragging their renewal date with them.
- **Per-config WebSocket paths (E1).** `pathname === '/'` made a per-user path impossible. The naive widening — accept an upgrade on any unreserved path — would answer 101 to an upgrade aimed anywhere, which announces the worker to anyone who tries one; the camouflage page exists so that everything not served looks like a web server. Only a path some enabled config claims as its `route_key` is a tunnel, matched on `ux_configs_route`; everything else still gets nginx, and a deleted config gives its path back. The lookup runs only for paths other than `/`, so the common case costs no extra read. Route precedence is covered in both directions (R6).

### Fixed
- **A renewed user was refused by the tunnel immediately.** `:renew` writes the legacy `expiry_days` mirror as *days remaining from now*, and the data plane measured it *from `created_at`* — so someone 29 days into a 30-day term who renewed for 15 more got `expiry_days = 16`, read as "expired sixteen days after creation", i.e. two weeks ago. The tunnel refused them on the spot while every screen showed fifteen days of credit; nothing in the API layer could see it, because only the data plane reads those columns. `studioExpiryAt` now prefers the absolute `expires_at` and falls back to the legacy pair only for a row that was never migrated.
- **Every usage figure in the product was frozen at the migration snapshot.** Admission reads the GB mirrors and those were being written, so quotas were enforced correctly — but `used_bytes` / `daily_used_bytes` were incremented by nothing after the v6 backfill, and those are what `Subscription-Userinfo` reports to the person's own client, what `/p/{token}` draws its bar from, what the user list shows, and what the dashboard counts for "over quota". Someone 25 GB into 30 was shown as having used nothing. Both families now move in the same UPDATE on all three flush paths — no extra D1 write.
- **The dashboard's 24-hour traffic tile always read zero.** It sums `usage_hourly`, which the migration created, the query was written against, and nothing ever wrote. Now written, coalesced in the isolate and flushed about once per 500 MB or when a session ends: it feeds a chart, not a limit, so a pending tail lost with an isolate is acceptable there and nowhere else.
- **The daily rollover cleared only the GB mirror**, leaving the byte counter — the one every screen reads — stuck at yesterday's total while the enforcing counter started again at zero.
- **The sync dropped people whenever a page ended on a tie.** `?since=` paged on `updated_at` alone: the last row's timestamp became the next watermark and the next request asked for rows strictly after it, so every row sharing that timestamp beyond the page limit was skipped permanently. The ties are routine — applying a plan writes one identical `updated_at` across every user on it, and the schema-6 backfill gave every legacy user the same value — so an account with more users on one timestamp than a page holds had a local index quietly missing them while looking complete. The watermark is now a keyset on `(updated_at, uid)` with an index in that shape (migration v10); an engine that does not send the tie-break keeps its exact previous behaviour, and the client detects a page that advances neither half and stops rather than looping.
- **Someone created from a plan did not get the plan's terms.** Only volume, duration and the device limit travelled; daily volume, connection limit, IP limit, reset policy and expiry mode were dropped at the point of creation even though the wire and the engine carry all of them. `Plan.toPolicy()` existed and had zero call sites. As a consequence "apply to the people on this plan" appeared to re-apply terms that had never been applied once.
- **The plan editor had no field for five of a plan's own terms**, so every plan made in the app had them null — and `:apply` includes them by default, which meant pressing it wrote those nulls over per-user settings. All five are now editable.
- **Deleting someone burned their username permanently.** A delete is a tombstone, `username` is UNIQUE, and the create path refused on any matching row — it even selected `deleted_at` and never used it. Silently, too: the local index drops tombstones, so the app reported the name as free and the engine then refused it. A tombstone now releases its name and stays a tombstone; a name held by a live user is still refused.
- **`GET /v1/users/{id}` returned 200 for a tombstone.** That is the call made before every mutation, so a deleted user could be re-read and painted on a detail screen as live until the write itself refused.
- **Every engine update or repair minted a new API key and never revoked the old one.** A fresh bootstrap secret is generated on each deploy because Cloudflare will not read a `secret_text` binding back, and the account row keeps only the newest key id — so the app forgot old keys while the engine went on honouring them. Ten repairs left ten live admin keys, none of them attributable to a device the operator still has. The previous key is now revoked once the new one is proven.
- **Discovery reported a worker it could not inspect as "nothing here."** When the bindings read failed the candidate fell through to a weak probe or to no result at all — and "no result" made the wizard offer to install, which is how a second engine is added beside one that was merely not visible. An uninspectable candidate is now reported as a failed examination, which the wizard already answers with a retry and no install button.
- **The plan drift line named accounts that had merely failed to answer.** Drift is now measured only against the accounts whose plan list was actually read; an unreachable account is reported once, as unreachable.
- **The engine address on the wizard's last step was an editable field that discarded edits**, and the one URL on that screen not pinned to LTR.
- **`StudioInstallation.accountId` held Cloudflare's account id** while every value it would be joined against — a user's installation id, a plan's presence set, the fleet's labels — holds the local record id. Harmless while nothing joined them, and wrong in the quiet way the moment the fleet screen does.
- **`checkStringParity` could not see a key defined twice in one catalogue**, because it built a `Set` before comparing. The duplicate failed several minutes later in `mergeDebugResources`, which names the file but not which of 3,400 keys is the new one.

### Changed
- **The connect bar’s wash moved only while connecting; green and red were paint.** The drift ran on `rememberInfiniteTransition`, which asks the window for a frame every vsync for as long as it is composed — whatever its value does, and even if nobody reads it — and this bar is on screen the whole time the config list is: 371 frames in six idle seconds, on the one screen that also has to scroll hundreds of rows. Freezing the movement outside the connecting state bought those frames by taking the light out of the two states the bar actually sits in, for hours at a time.
  The clock is thrown away instead of the movement. It advances on `delay`, which requests no frame at all — the loop only writes a float; it is read inside the `Canvas` draw lambda, so a tick invalidates one display list rather than recomposing anything; and it ticks every 64ms rather than at vsync, roughly a quarter of the frames. That rate is affordable because one cycle of this wash is three minutes: replaying the file’s own noise over the real geometry, a light’s centre moves ~4px between ticks and 20px at its steepest, across a radial gradient with a 542px falloff and no edge anywhere. `DriftingLights` loses its `animate` parameter, which existed only as this mitigation.
  **Fixed on the way: the wash teleported every three minutes.** The clock wrapped at `CYCLE_MS`, but `noise` is hashed on an integer lattice and has no period, so `noise(CYCLE * 0.55)` and `noise(0)` are unrelated — all four lights jumped 617px at once, against per-tick steps of about 20. Latent since the file was ported (`infiniteRepeatable` restarts the same way) and never seen, because the drift only ever ran for the few seconds a tunnel takes to come up. The wrap was there for float precision, and is not needed for it: measured in `Float` at a 24-hour session, the largest step is 28px and the clock’s rate is 0.7% off ideal — it degrades by running imperceptibly fast, never by jumping.
- **About moved off the home screen and into Settings, as the last row of the About group.** A tile on the icon grid said this was a destination on the level of a transport, when it is the one screen nobody opens twice — and it was holding one of the grid’s 24 slots to say it. It is `Settings › About › About Us` now: the last row of the same group that already carries the version, the software updater and the crash reports, which is already the answer to every question this screen answers. Last rather than first, because the three rows above it are things a user comes to Settings to *do*; this one is reading. The row takes a `Groups` glyph rather than the `Info` it carried on the grid, since the version row two above it is already an `Info` glyph and two identical marks in one card is the one place the rows stop being distinguishable at a glance.
  On a tablet, a television or a DeX window it **splits into two panes like the rest of Settings**: About and the changelog render in the detail pane while the settings list stays put beside them. That is what the refactor is for — the changelog is no longer opened by a `showChangelog` flag held inside `AboutScreen`, it is `P_ABOUT_CHANGELOG` on the settings route stack, so both pages go through the same `pane(routeKey)` the rest of the tree does and physical back walks Changelog → About → Settings on one stack instead of two handlers. `ChangelogScreen` became public and now reads its own releases for the same reason.
  A saved icon layout is left alone: `HomeLayoutStore.load` reconciles saved ids against `GRID_DEFAULT` through `mapNotNull`, so the removed id drops out of an arrangement the user dragged themselves and leaves no gap behind it. `AboutScreen` is no longer a tab — the `"about"` entry is gone from `SELF_HEADED` and from `AppScreen`’s tab hosts, and `R.string.home_about` with it.

### Added
- **Iran configs and domain fronting each get a home-screen icon, and connect from there.** Both were folders inside the config list, which is the wrong shelf for either: that list is where configs *arrive* — a panel deployed, a subscription imported, a scan combined — and these two ship with the app, have nothing to deploy, and have no relationship to the Cloudflare account or the panels the rest of that screen is about. The Iran defaults in particular are what someone reaches for on the day nothing else works, and "open the config list and find the right folder among the panels" is not a path anyone walks in that state.
  One screen serves both, since they are the same shape: a short list of built-in JSON profiles, tap a row to connect and tap again to stop, with the live state read from the service rather than a local flag (they can also be started from the config list or the tile). Iran carries the local-port notice the config list already showed inside its folder — those files hardcode inbound 10808, so in proxy mode a changed local port makes them look broken when the setting is. Domain fronting hosts its full setup wizard *above* the list, because its config cannot carry anything until the certificate it terminates TLS with is trusted, and a connect button above an unfinished wizard is a button whose only outcome is failure. Both go through `ScanGuard` like every other connect path, and `ActiveEngines` now maps `default_mlmvpn_*` and the fronting node to the new icons instead of letting them light the V2Ray lamp — which sent anyone who tapped it to a list with the connected config hidden in a folder.
  Both are also **removed from the config list**, where they had been folders. `visibleNodes` now filters them out alongside the SNI configs, which also takes the two cards that lived inside those folders with them — the Iran local-port notice and the domain-fronting setup card, both of which moved to the new screens. The filter is applied to what the screen SHOWS, not to what it stores: the configs are untouched on disk and still connectable, and since every write on that screen now merges by id rather than replacing the list, filtering the view can no longer delete them (which is exactly what it would have done before that fix).

### Performance
- **The app redrew itself at ~60fps forever on any screen carrying the connect card.** The colour wash ported from NetDoctor is built on `rememberInfiniteTransition`, which requests a frame every vsync for as long as it is in composition — whatever its target value, and even if nobody reads it. `AppScreen.kt` already carries a comment warning about precisely this from the last time it happened. Measured with `dumpsys gfxinfo`, six idle seconds untouched: **371 frames on the config screen, 58 on the home screen**. The transition is now created only when it is actually animating, and the card animates only while connecting. Idle frames on the config screen: **371 → 0**.
- **Readings from a bulk test appeared only when the run ended.** Each result was published by replacing the entire list, and this screen derives heavily from that list: an SNI filter that parses every config's URI, a comparator sort, then a panel/folder filter — on the order of a hundred thousand URI parses and three hundred full sorts across one run of 318 configs, all on the main thread. Results now go into snapshot maps that the ROW reads inside its own composition, so one reading invalidates one row; the list is rebuilt and saved once by `flushLive()` when the run ends. This is what the SNI config list already did, which is why that screen was never affected. Verified across a 318-config run: pending rows 8 → 7 → 2 and readings 8 → 9 → 14 while still in progress.
  Not fixed, and measured rather than guessed: scrolling this list still costs ~97ms a frame. By experiment — with the per-row frosted glass 97ms, without it 30ms, without only the wallpaper blit 73ms. An optimisation of the glass draw stack (four constant veils composited once, the rim drawn inline instead of by `Modifier.border`, bilinear sampling) produced **no measurable change** and was reverted rather than left in the app's most-used visual primitive on the strength of a plausible story. The remaining cost is per-row composition, which needs its own pass.

### Fixed
- **The test counter counted configs the screen was hiding.** One config visible and delay reported two; three visible and it reported four. Each of the three bulk tests rebuilt its own target list from the full store with its own copy of the panel/folder rule, and the store still contains the SNI configs, which this screen filters out (`visibleNodes`). The extras were the hidden SNI copies of the same panel — measured invisibly, and over a path that cannot work for them since the SNI front is not in the way. All three now take `displayedNodes`, the same list the `LazyColumn` renders, so the count, the rows and the work are one set. The duplicated filter expression went with it.

### Changed
- **The config list no longer jumps into the middle when a test starts.** Pressing delay marks every target row `…`, which is not a reading, so the sort key of the whole list vanished in one frame and it reshuffled — and LazyColumn anchors the viewport to the first visible item by key, so the rows under the user slid away. The order is frozen for the duration of a run and the list scrolls to the top; the freeze lifts and it sorts once at the end. The sort button now also takes the user to the top.
- **Two jump arrows on the config list.** Scrolling down offers the end, scrolling up offers the top, following the gesture rather than the position; each hides at the end it would point to. Direction comes from a single monotonic position rather than comparing `firstVisibleItemIndex` and `firstVisibleItemScrollOffset` separately — the offset restarts at zero whenever the index changes, which reported "up" on the very frame a new row came into view while scrolling down. The jump covers the distance in one step and animates only the arrival: `animateScrollToItem` over three hundred rows does not animate the journey, it approximates and corrects, which arrives as the list lurching forward in blocks.
- **The SNI config list wears the connection screen's toolbar**: delay, sort, delete-the-dead and delete-all, at the top of the page. This is a list of configs, and everything anyone wants to do with a list of configs is measure it, order it and take things out of it; it used to be a list you could only pick from, with two deletions parked underneath where they were found last. The two destructive labels say "delete" in full — a four-character label over a bin icon is only obvious to whoever wrote it, and these are the buttons where being wrong costs most. Sorting is fastest-first by default and puts unanswered configs after the fast ones and unmeasured ones last, because a config with no reading is not a slow config, it has not been asked.
- **One button does both halves of the SNI measurement.** "Measure and pick the fastest" now runs a TLS handshake to every entry point and adopts the fastest that answered, then measures each config through that entry point and adopts the fastest of those. They were two buttons, which put the burden of knowing the order on the user: measuring configs over a slow entry point measures the entry point, and adopting a faster one afterwards throws every number away.
- **Deleting one SNI config is a swipe now**, the way a call list does it — drag the row aside and the bin appears under it. Deleting one thing belongs on the thing: a toolbar button has to act on "the selected one", so deleting meant selecting first, and selecting is also how you choose what to connect. An open row closes on a tap rather than selecting, so a thumb landing wide of the bin cannot pick the config by accident.
- **Two buttons left the connection screen.** The bulk download-speed test is gone: it pulled a real download through every config in a list routinely hundreds long, minutes of traffic to answer a question the delay test beside it already answers well enough to sort by. The per-config version stays on the config's own page. And building SNI configs has left the "+" menu — it lives in the SNI section with the engine, the list and the connection, and two doors into one operation are two places that have to be kept in step forever.

### Fixed
- **Measurements appeared only when the whole run finished, on both screens, for two different reasons.** On the SNI list the results were accumulated and written in one go after the last config; each is published the moment it is known now. On the connection screen the numbers were arriving and being *erased*: `nodes` was seeded with `remember(nodesFlowState) { mutableStateOf(nodesFlowState) }`, so every save re-seeded the whole screen from disk — and a bulk delay test saves as it goes (a country lookup lands, a result is merged), each of those resetting every row still showing `...` back to whatever the disk last held. Membership now comes from the store and the three measurement fields from the screen, which is the only place that knows them yet.
  The SNI list also re-orders as readings land, fastest climbing to the top. That was wrong once too, and subtly: the sorted list was held in `remember(configs, delays, sortByDelay)`, and `delays` is a snapshot map whose INSTANCE never changes, so the key never changed and the order computed on the first frame was the order you kept. Sorting a few hundred rows on each recomposition costs nothing next to being wrong.
  Verified on the device across a 158-config run: readings visible at 53/158, the order changing between samples as faster configs arrived (118,122,122… → 118,120,121…), ascending throughout.

- **Configs deleted themselves, and it was also the app's most reported crash.** Reported from the device: 180 SNI configs were built, and on returning to the app they had all gone. The cause is the same one behind the crash that dominates the fleet's reports. Twelve places recorded a small change — a ping result, a rename, one deletion — by doing `nodeManager.nodes.clear(); nodeManager.nodes.addAll(screenSnapshot); saveNodes()`, which replaces the ENTIRE stored list with whatever that screen happened to be holding.
  Two distinct failures follow. First, a measurement knows something about the configs it measured and nothing whatsoever about the rest, so expressing its result as a whole-list replacement deletes everything added while it was running — and a bulk test runs for minutes while building SNI configs on another screen takes seconds. Second, `clear()` and `addAll()` are two operations, not one: `nodes` is a `Collections.synchronizedList`, so each call is individually safe and the pair is not. Two of them interleaving as clear, clear, addAll(A), addAll(B) leaves the list holding **every node twice with the same id**, which LazyColumn answers by throwing `IllegalArgumentException: Key "<uuid>" was already used` on its next measure pass — while measuring, so the screen that would let the user fix it is the screen that will not open. Of the 100 crash signatures on the dashboard for the last 30 days, roughly 85 are that one error (each carrying a different uuid, which is why it groups as many signatures rather than one), spanning 1.2.31 and 1.2.32 continuously.
  `NodeManager` now owns the writes: `replaceAll` (atomic clear+fill), `mergeById` (write these rows back by id, leave every other row alone, and ignore an id that is no longer there so a result cannot resurrect a deleted config) and `removeByIds`, each holding the list's lock across the whole operation. All twelve call sites in `NodesTab` were converted — per-node results and both bulk finals merge, group delete and cleanup remove by id. A second guard sits underneath: `saveNodes` drops duplicate ids before writing, because a duplicate that reaches disk was not only a crash on every launch but a silent data loss — `loadNodes` de-duplicates by id on the way in, so whichever copy came second was quietly deleted.

- **The shared config pool went down for everyone, and the cause was one clause.** On 2026-09-06 every endpoint of the pool worker began answering `D1_ERROR: your account has exceeded D1's free tier daily row read limit`. That ceiling is account-wide, so `/list`, `/stats` and `/crashes` fell over together — and a worker that cannot read its database has nothing to serve. D1 bills rows a query **looks at**, not rows it returns, and the trial-slot query ended in `ORDER BY RANDOM()`, which no index can satisfy: SQLite has to materialise every matching row before it can sort, so `LIMIT 6` saved nothing. Its filter was `state = 'probation'` with no time bound, and probation is the default state of every config anyone has ever reported — so each call read every unproven row ever recorded for that ISP, and the cost grew with the table forever. Nothing ever deleted a row, so the table only grew.
  The sample now comes from a random point on the hash ring: `h` is a sha256 of the config URI and is therefore already uniform, so walking `(isp, state, h)` forward from a random cursor is the same random sample for the price of the rows it returns — with a second read from the start of the ring so a cursor landing near the end does not under-sample the tail. Measured in SQLite against a 60,000-row table with 20,000 rows on the ISP being served: **378,760 VDBE steps before, 111 after**, and unlike the old query that figure does not grow with the table. The band query was split so it names one state at a time (`state IN ('active','probation')` with `ORDER BY last_ok` cannot use the third index column and sorts everything; one state at a time, the index IS the order) — 12,006 steps to 2,055, and now hard-capped at 200 active plus 50 probation rows. The quarantine retry became an oldest-failure-first queue that rotates itself, since retrying rewrites `last_bad`.
  Two things were added alongside. A **sweep** rides roughly one report in forty, after the response, deleting up to 400 rows a time: quarantined rows untouched for a week, unproven rows untouched for three days, crash reports older than a month. Nothing `active` is ever touched, and a deleted stats row is not lost data — `configs` keeps the URI and the next measurement re-creates it. And the **stats dashboard is served from KV for five minutes**: every figure on it is an aggregate over the whole table, so a tab left refreshing was rescanning everything, several times, per load. `?fresh=1` forces a rescan.

### Added
